- Any path from F to M must enter the main graph through one of F’s three incident edges: via E directly (F-E-M), via G then toward E/H… (F-G-...-M), or via K/X/G cluster back to G then onward. All these eventually must pass through E or J to reach M, because M’s only neighbors besides L are E and J; L connects only to E and M (triangle E-L-M). Thus every path to M must use either edge (E, M) or reach J then use (J, M), or reach L then (L, M). However, reaching L requires E (since L connects only to E and M), so any L-based route still needs (E, L) then (L, M); cutting (E, M) does not block E-L-M because (L, M) provides connection; but to reach L from F, the path must go through E-L; thus to block all, we can cut (M, E) and (M, J) (leaving L unreachable to M because it would still be E-L-M but uses (M, L) edge). Actually if we cut (M,E) and (M,J) but leave (M,L), there exists F-...-E-L-M path using (M,L). So must cut (M,L) OR cut (E,L). Minimal cut is to remove edges incident to M: remove (M,E) and (M,J) and (M,L) — that isolates M with 3 edges.

Can we do with 2 edges? Is there an edge cut elsewhere of size 2 separating M from F? To connect to M you must reach either E or J or L and then traverse one of (E,M), (J,M), (L,M). By removing two edges not incident to M, could we separate F from both E and J and L simultaneously with 2 edges? F has three disjoint entrances: F-E, F-G, F-K; but K connects to G and X etc which connect to G; from G there is an edge to H and to F and to X and K; from G-H to E/B etc eventually to E/J. There is also direct F-E. To block all paths from F to {E,J,L} with two edges seems hard because F has two edge-disjoint paths to E: direct (F,E) and via F-G-H-E; plus via F-G-H-I-J to J etc. By Menger, edge-connectivity likely 3 (need at least 3 cuts). Easiest is isolate M by cutting its 3 edges. Therefore minimal is 3.

Verify: Removing (M,E), (M,J), (M,L) severs all paths since M has no remaining edges. Any smaller set of 2 edges would leave at least one of these edges intact, yielding a path from F to that neighbor (E or J or L): 
- F to E exists via direct (F,E) or via F-G-H-E.
- F to J exists via F-E-...-I-J or F-G-H-I-J.
- F to L exists via F-E-L.
Thus 2 edges cannot disconnect.

Final Answer:

- Enumerate independent route groups from N to G:
  1) Direct lower route: N–B–H–G.
  2) Upper route via E/F: N–B–E–F–X–K–G (or N–C–B–E… same once at B/E).
  3) Left detours rejoin at B: N–Y–O–P–I–B–… and N–C–I–B–…; these still must pass through either edge (B,H) or the chain from B to E then to G.

- Therefore, any N→G path must traverse one of two bottleneck edges leaving the B/E area toward G:
  - Edge (B, H) to reach G from below, or
  - Edge (F, X) or (X, K) or (K, G) along the upper branch starting from E/F.
  But from B/E to the upper branch, the only exit toward G is through edge (F, X) (since E connects to L/M/B only). So cutting (F, X) severs all upper-route possibilities.

Minimal cut:
- Remove (B, H) to break the lower path.
- Remove (F, X) to break the upper path.
- After removing both, check all alternatives:
  - Any path reaching H from left cannot reach G because (B, H) is gone and there’s no other entry to H from the left/top except the large outer frame which still requires (B, H) to continue to G.
  - Any attempt to go via E/F to the top right is blocked at (F, X); with (F, X) removed there is no connection from the left side (through E/F) to K/G.
  - Thus no path from N (through Y/C/B/E networks) can reach G.

No single edge suffices: if only (B, H) is removed, N–…–B–E–F–X–K–G remains. If only (F, X) is removed, N–B–H–G remains.

Final Answer:

- Try size-1 cut: removing any single node in {M, D, P} fails (e.g., remove M, path J–D–P–O–…–E–F remains; remove D, path J–M–…–E–F remains; remove P, path J–M–…–E–F remains).

  - {D, P} works: J’s only remaining neighbor is M; M’s route to E→F still exists, so {D, P} alone does NOT disconnect.
- Therefore, the minimal effective 2-node cut is {M, P}. Verify:
  - After removing M and P, J has only D; D connects only to J (since its edge to P is gone). Thus no path from J to any other node, so no path to F.
- No 1-node cut works, and a 2-node cut exists, so the minimum is 2.

- Could a single-node cut work? Removing H still leaves paths G–E–B–C–I, or G–F–X–K–O–P–I, etc. Removing C leaves G–H–I. Removing P leaves G–H–I or G–…–C–I. So any single node fails.

- Remove H and C: Then paths via P remain (e.g., G–E–B–C? blocked; but G–F–X–K–O–P–I or G–E–M–J–P–I). So need to also block P if H removed? Not necessary if we choose a pair that blocks both C and P.

   • Direct G–H–I broken.
   • Any route via C: G→E/B→C→I exists; but C still connects to I, so H+P is insufficient.

- Remove C and P: 
   • Then I can only be reached from G via H, but H remains; G–H–I exists. So insufficient.

- Remove H plus one of C or P is insufficient as shown.

Hence minimum cut must include H and also block the other entrance (C or P) AND the remaining one as well; i.e., we need both C and P removed in addition to H.

Verify 3-node cut [H, C, P]:
- Any path must approach I via H, C, or P. Removing all three eliminates all entries to I; no other neighbor connects to I.
- Check there isn’t any hidden direct edge to I from J or O: I has visible edges to H, C, P only. There is an edge I–J? Not shown. I–O? Not shown. So safe.

Minimality:
- With 2 nodes, at least one of H, C, P would remain, yielding a path (either G–H–I or G–…–C–I or G–…–P–I). Therefore 2 is insufficient.
